In prehistory, we're often dealing with hypotheses, patterns, and best guesses based on limited evidence of the past. Often, it's not about absolute truth, rather about reasoned explanation. Most of the times from these lectures, you're learning how to think historically to understand those explanations. Because real learning isn’t just about certainty. It’s also about understanding complexity.
And more importantly, a lecture, even with some bias or flaws, is far more organized, thoughtful, and evidence-based than TikTok or Reddit posts.

I don’t think a lot of people in the comments understand the role of education.

Professor is TEACHING critical thinking as a skill, THROUGH the medium of civilisational history.

Even if you don’t agree with the theories laid out in the video.
 
The crux of the lesson is to:

1. Challenge our everyday understanding of human nature (what is today, wasn’t 10,000 years ago)

2. Critically evaluate theories and devise one’s own opinion.

No one will ever be 100% correct about the agrarian past, it is about  understanding differing concepts.

The fact so many people in the comments can’t grasp this shows a failure in our education systems.

I think its important to view this lecture with a grain of salt. Marija Gimbutas was undoubtedly a monolith in her field with the Kurgan Hypothesis, but its also good to note that there hasn't been any significant direct evidence to support her hypothesis that the people of Old Europe were particularly peaceful or matrifocal.

This is not to say that all Old European societies were patriarchal or violent, but rather, that the truth probably lies somewhere around the middle, which is that Old Europe had a mix of various different cultures and beliefs, and was probably a very diverse place. 

Not trying to debate with anyone here since i'm no historian/anthropologist, but it is a fact that Marija Gimbutas' works were written decades ago and we've seen a lot of differing perspectives since then. 

That being said, wonderful lecture from Professor Jiang, all respect and love

>
> @haktwoah Sure! I can share a bunch of links, but the gist is this- all men carry genetic information on their Y chromosome and in the mitochondria that is passed down- the Y chromosome information is passed down patrilineally, so from your father's father's father... and so forth, and the mitochondrial DNA is matrilineal. These are called haplotypes. There are relatively few Y chromosomal haplotypes, but a huge range of diverse female ones, especially during early agriculture, but there was also an imbalance pre-agriculture, in hunter-gatherer societies. This suggests that only a small number of men mated, but almost all women mated during these time periods.   
>   
> Additionally, in extant hunter-gatherer societies in remote parts of the world today, these are almost entirely monogamous or polygynous. The few examples of polyandry like described in this video have actually been extremely poor post-agricultural societies.   
>

### **@cassidycross3406** | 👍 35 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaT_ZUDjHrM&lc=UgwZbxjYn_UzZRLXCnp4AaABAg)

I took a gender studies class that was critical of Maria Gimbutas, J. J. Bachofen, Engels, and Nietzche. The final argument, in brief, was that a matriarchal prehistory myths were established by patriarchal structures to explain how first women had all the power, lost it due to their lack of leadership qualities, and why men are now the rightful leaders. 

These myths were basically a way to explain to prehistoric women why women were not meant to be leaders and had already their chance and failed. 

Yet, on the other hand, the idea of a matriarchy gave modern feminists the push to see patriarchy as contingent rather than necessary, inspiring a movement. 

Gimbutas's methodology is highly questionable, especially her use of symbols, as fun as it may be. I'm still undecided about her.

I love how Mr Jiang knits all these different ideas together to form a narrative base. And I also love the way he challenges a lot of the dominant narrative of today. 

However, this episode I'm not so sure about... It feels a bit as wishful thinking, I'm not sure we've changed all that much as a species. 

Yes there were also some groups of people doing things very differently, (matriarchy) but would this be the case for most of the humans at that time? And how much of that would depend on the (technological) capabilities of those people and the limitations of their environment?
Probably the truth lies somewhere in the middle? Maybe the humans on average were different back them but not as starkly different as he is describing?

And maybe if the ancient people were that different, how much of this correlates with modern humans? Maybe we've evolved in a way these old structures of society are not longer possible? (So maybe it's only relevant in this narrative of where we come from?)

Speaking as a woman, I've experienced much sabotage from other women. And I've seen it happen to many other women too. 
Sadly most women view other women as competition, I've never experienced any sisterhood-like thing with women I wasn't already "affiliated" with... (In a best friend, sister type of way so not that often)

This lies deeper than "just" a social construct imho. 
In my experience, men aren much more capable of working together in a large group. Women always form smaller groups and fight with each other.

Maybe this is the point; for war a large group is necessary. Women can't form large groups and therefore less war 😄

> Jealousy is an evolutionary mesa optimizer, meaning that it is an instinct which indirectly optimizes for a certain statistical outcome, without directly pointing to it. The preferred outcome of any gene in the pool is to keep being expressed. Individuals which experience jealousy will be more competitive about their reproduction and resource management, giving them an advantage in terms of staying in the gene pool. They don't need to understand why it's helpful, the same way that you don't need to understand genes in order to be afraid of heights. Your fear of heights got selected and is very prominent within the population precisely because it protects you from dying, which increases the chances that your genes will live on. No need for a meta understanding of this instinct for it to operate effectively.   
>   
> Thinking critically isn't taking one framework for granted and just going with it. Rather understanding it, then examining through the lens of as many other knowledge bases you have.   
>   
> Just because he clearly states his sources doesn't mean it's necessarily true or even sound, it just means that he's honest.  
>   
> Be open minded in the sense that you're willing to examine any idea fairly, but open mindedness doesn't mean having your mind so open that any shit can enter.

Rather, it reflects an economic discrepancy within the current social paradigm. It is much easier for a woman to obtain sex than it is for the average man; therefore, when a man has acquired many sexual partners, he is often seen as a “stud,” whereas a woman is labelled a “slut.”

This dynamic is less about morality and more about supply and demand. Men tend to desire what is new or untouched, and from this preference we can extrapolate that, within the current zeitgeist, women are treated—to some extent—as goods or products. At the same time, it is important to recognise that “studs” are also what many women desire. Women are often drawn to men with optionality because, within this paradigm, optionality signals abundance of material wealth, security, and perceived safety. Taken together, these standards are a reflection of the society and economic framework in which we live, rather than an arbitrary moral inconsistency.

Please challenge my viewpoint and let me learn from you, too.

### **@leviathanv3135**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaT_ZUDjHrM&lc=Ugz5gjIDaz-IE8d_TcV4AaABAg)

⸻

I agree with a lot of what you’re saying overall, especially the idea that environment shapes culture, symbolism, and religion. Where I hesitate is when this turns into a kind of “peaceful egalitarian Old Europe vs. violent patriarchy” story, because that feels like replacing one myth with another.

I think of evolution less as random chaos and more like a domino effect shaped by environment and biological constraints. I don’t see that as conflicting with spirituality at all. For me, God created the laws of the universe, and evolution is simply the process by which those laws unfold over time. I actually take the Gnostic idea of the Monad seriously — one source, many expressions — and I see gods and mythologies as localized ways different cultures encounter and express that same underlying reality.

Cultures aren’t arbitrary social constructs. They’re adaptive responses to environment and biology. Pastoral, milk-drinking nomads tend to end up with sky or thunder gods. Jungle societies lean toward animism or polytheism. Farming societies emphasize fertility and goddesses. That doesn’t mean one system was morally superior — it means they were shaped by different conditions and had different “homework,” so to speak. Symbolism doesn’t automatically translate into political prescriptions.

I’m very much in favor of egalitarianism — equal worth and shared responsibility — but I don’t think that requires denying biology. Reproduction, pregnancy, and long-term parental investment place real constraints on how societies organize themselves. We’re highly imaginative and creative, but we didn’t escape our evolutionary inheritance. Coming from apes, we still carry deep, unconscious structures around pair-bonding and paternity, whether we like it or not.

I also think it’s important to separate Darwin from later ideological misuse. A lot of the moral baggage people attach to evolution didn’t come from Charles Darwin, but from Herbert Spencer. Spencer took evolution and turned it into “survival of the fittest” as a social and moral justification for hierarchy, merit, and even racism. But that kind of “merit” is mostly based on biology and environment — traits you don’t choose. Evolution describes a process; it doesn’t reward virtue or justify domination.

So for me, evolution doesn’t deny God at all. It explains the process by which life becomes complex enough to express imagination, art, myth, and spiritual awareness in the first place

### **@leviathanv3135**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaT_ZUDjHrM&lc=UgyrB96ublXpfVP2FIF4AaABAg)

39:00

I think a lot of this discussion skips some important biology. Humans are most closely related to chimpanzees and bonobos, with gorillas a bit farther back and monkeys like rhesus macaques much more distant. Bonobos are interesting, but their social and sexual behavior evolved under very specific ecological conditions that don’t match human evolutionary pressures.

Humans aren’t bonobos — but we aren’t pure dominance apes either. We’re moderately sexually dimorphic: not as extreme as gorillas, but more than bonobos. That alone suggests a mixed evolutionary strategy. Sexual dimorphism means different reproductive costs, especially pregnancy, childbirth, and long infant dependency. Those pressures existed long before culture.

As human brains and heads got larger, childbirth became riskier and infancy much longer. That made cooperation, pair-bonding, protection, provisioning, and planning biologically necessary, not culturally optional. Managing those costs favored social intelligence — which helps explain why human brains expanded.

Paternity concern also isn’t a cultural invention. It’s a biological reality tied to internal fertilization and long-term investment. Culture doesn’t create that concern — it regulates and expresses it in different ways.

So explaining everything as “men wanting to control women” ignores sexual dimorphism, life-history constraints, and evolutionary trade-offs. At the same time, using biology to justify dominance or entitlement is also wrong. Biology describes constraints, not moral hierarchies. Culture is a response to biology — not its replacement.

### **@leviathanv3135**

38:31


I think both extremes get this wrong, just in different ways. Some feminist explanations reduce everything to men wanting control and treat sexual norms as purely cultural, which ignores basic evolutionary biology and reproductive asymmetry. But a lot of alpha-male takes go wrong in the opposite direction by treating biology as a moral justification for dominance or entitlement.

Biology describes constraints, not values. Pregnancy, long childhood dependency, and parental investment created real evolutionary pressures on both sexes, and cultures evolved norms to manage those pressures. That doesn’t justify shaming or oppression, and it also doesn’t justify ego or superiority narratives.

Culture is a response to biology, not its replacement — and turning biology into ideology, from either side, just distorts the science.

Disproval of promiscuity in women is not based in some dominance fantasy, but the fact that children cost a lot of resources and that men don't want to work themselves to death for another mans offspring. That may be irrelevant in a culture in which people share what they hunted down anyways since they can not store it. At one moment a deer carcass is a big chunk of meat in the next it is a smelly health hazard that attracts predators. So people shared their loot since they couldn't keep it and stored it as fat on other people who would hopefully soon return the favor. With animal husbandry and grain (living animals are a form of food storage and grain can be stored for decades) suddenly private property becomes a thing. As soon as the food storage problem was transcended, people became territorial and the masculine took over. Women became picky with whom to mate, based on who could provide the most calories.

### **@FlaiderX** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaT_ZUDjHrM&lc=UgxgTQtR4KdLoRcFcG54AaABAg)

The weakest possible article. I don't know why she's being praised here at all...

First, the idea that there was once a single proto-language has been criticized for a long time. This is an area of discussion, not facts. And the author of the lecture doesn't try to mention it at all. The existence of a proto-language has not been proven. From the same point of view, paleolinguistics and a number of authors who promote the idea of protolanguage are criticized. The fact that some words sound consonant and can mean the same thing is not proof of the existence of a proto-language. These can be either coincidences or borrowings from each other. It's a complicated topic, and I'm not an expert, but I remember exactly how active she is in her discussions. 

Secondly, Maria Gimbutas never seemed to say that societies were run by women. She used the term "Matrifocal," which meant that the mother was in the center. 

Third, I am amused by the way he describes three reasons why Maria Gimbutas was laughed at. Moreover, he immediately starts accusing men. They laughed at her because there were a lot of men in science. He ignores the fact that some of the most widely cited critical articles and books that criticized her ideas were written by women and feminists. The articles from 1980-1990 clearly cover this. This bias against men will also appear at the end of the lecture. Next, he decided to say that they were laughing at her, because the idea of a peaceful past or the fact that the wives were leaders was ridiculous. But this is nonsense. There have been many great female rulers in historical patriarchal cultures. Wars in the modern sense may not have happened in the past, but there certainly were conflicts. 

As a result, we can already see his bias here, because he ignores the real reason for not accepting some of Maria Gimbutas' ideas. I will note that her Kurgan hypothesis was accepted (now called the Steppe hypothesis). She is mostly criticized for her claims about the EXCEPTIONAL peacefulness of ancient societies (namely, that they were exceptionally peaceful without hints of violence). She has also been criticized for her research methodology and her Goddess hypothesis. The latter is especially interesting because according to her methodology, any symbol is almost always associated with a goddess and a woman. Chevron, miander, circle, butterfly, whatever. Those who read her books could understand and see it when the author of the book simply claims that it is a symbol of the goddess and that's it. Why the author of the lecture ignores this is not clear to me, because I personally read these articles and their arguments are generally strong and have a scientific and logical basis. 

I also wonder what DNA evidence proves a military invasion? As far as I know, in 2017 they only proved that there was migration. Moreover, more modern studies may show a more complex picture of how migration was reciprocal and bidirectional. DNA proves a part of the Kurgan hypothesis, where they talked about migration. There's not a word about military conquests. 


I'm just surprised by some anthropology and scientists. Everyone talks all the time about what incredible societies there were in the past. There were no organized wars, etc. The author himself tries to explain this in the end by saying that women cooperate better, etc. But in general, the idea of demographic differences in societies does not occur to their unreasonable heads. The societies of the "Mother Goddess" and at the time of these societies (they did not exist for the most part because there was a diversity of societies, and not a single society of the Mother Goddess), the population density was incredibly low. So much so that there were no motivators for wars in the mass. Societies were not large in number and the density of people (of all tribes) was not large, i.e. the struggle for land, food, etc. was not justified. We can clearly trace the exponential growth of the human population and the increase in the density of wars due to the increase in the population density of our planet. Of course, this is a hypothesis. But for some reason, such an obvious thought did not occur to him.

In the end, it immediately became clear which ideas he was committed to. It's some kind of feminism or misandry. Men are worse leaders because they are competitive, unable to empathize, cooperate, and women could subjugate them through indirect forms of aggression (gossip). It's very funny to read this. Although the differences between the groups are of an average nature and, in general, these differences are not large within the framework of modern science, the lecturer's main mistake is that he takes some data (and they are contradictory, i.e. he deals with biased coverage of science) obtained on populations of modern humans and extrapolates them to ancient societies. We don't know what kind of men and women there were 20,000 years ago. 

As a result, the lecture itself is weak. There are some interesting reflections. But the claims that men desperately needed to control women's sexuality, etc., are schizophrenic nonsense. Why doesn't he mention the presence of (some) polygyny in matrilineal societies? He mentioned polyandry, but not polygyny.

The examples of polygamous Hunter-Gatherer (HG) cultures you use isn't representative of most HGs. Humans have always mostly practiced Serial Monogamy. Polyandry is extremely rare making up less than 1% of traditional societies. Indo-Europeans (IE) were more polygamous, specifically, polygynous than the Old Europeans, who weren't peaceful, egalitarian, & Matriarchal, though may have seemed to be because of how violent, hierarchical, & Patriarchal IEs were. Even tribal Mother Goddess cults usually have a priest class restricted to males only. Shamans are almost always men. Monogamy is the norm not because of private property, but because it's the best way to ensure peace & guarantee paternity. The few societies that allow Polyandry, like Tibetans, only do so due to a lack of women which results in brothers having to share a wife. We have twice as many female ancestors than male, meaning that when Polygamy was practiced, it was usually Polygyny & not Polyandry.

40:48  no no no you’re wrong. The evidence doesn’t support this. The difference between men and women it’s not cultural it’s biological. How did you think we end up with larger brains? The problem with this framing is that it ignores what a larger human brain actually implies. Humans didn’t just evolve bigger brains — we evolved larger heads, riskier childbirth, earlier birth, and extremely long childhood dependency. Those are biological constraints, not cultural ones.

Once you account for sexual dimorphism and the costs of pregnancy and infant care, cooperation, pair-bonding, and concern with paternity become biologically relevant. Culture didn’t invent those pressures — it evolved to manage them.

A species with extreme encephalization cannot be explained by “differences are mostly cultural.” Larger brains make biology matter more, not less.
